People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brought me in is… I just feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Like, especially when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do things that should be straightforward, like paperwork or trying to go over shared household plans, I feel this restless, uncomfortable need to move or shift or get up. It’s not even always that the task is hard, exactly — it’s more like my body won’t cooperate with the idea of staying still and calmly handling it.

And it’s been bothering me because those are normal adult things, you know? Sitting down, sorting through forms, talking through plans at home, making decisions without feeling like I’m crawling out of my skin. I keep thinking I should be able to just sit there and get through it, but instead I feel agitated and unsettled, like I’m physically unable to relax into it. That’s the main reason I wanted to come in.

the calendar without really taking a break, like I’m being pushed from the inside.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the pharmacy or sitting until it’s my turn to speak in a committee meeting makes me tense and fidgety, and I catch myself checking the time over and over.\n- key: respectful, but I still jump into my husband’s conversations or finish people’s thoughts because the response comes out before I’ve made myself wait.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: manifestation: The fallout has been that people at home and in my volunteer circles can’t reliably count on me, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I keep letting things fall year and five months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 41 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S012\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in — I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brought you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is… I just feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. Like, especially when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do things that should be straightforward, like paperwork or trying to go over shared household plans, I feel this restless, uncomfortable need to move or shift or get up. It’s not even always that the task is hard, exactly — it’s more like my body won’t cooperate with the idea of staying still and calmly handling it.\n\nAnd it’s been bothering me because those are normal adult things, you know? Sitting down, sorting through forms, talking through plans at home, making decisions without feeling like I’m crawling out of my skin. I keep thinking I should be able to just sit there and get through it, but instead I feel agitated and unsettled, like I’m physically unable to relax into it. That’s the main reason I wanted to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any medications recently, especially antipsychotics or anti-nausea drugs, before these restless feelings began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. At least, nothing comes to mind like that. I haven’t had some obvious “this started after I began a new pill” kind of thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m trying to think through it because I know sometimes there are medications people don’t think of as relevant, but I’m not aware of starting or changing anything like an antipsychotic or an anti-nausea medication before this. I don’t want to say something too absolute if I’m forgetting some tiny thing, but no, I haven’t noticed a medication change connected to the restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, did you notice similar restlessness or trouble sitting still and focusing on tasks when you were a child or teenager, or is this something that's only started more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely wasn’t only adulthood. Looking back, it was already kind of… part of me in elementary school. I was that girl with the messy desk where papers were just shoved in everywhere, and I’d forget permission slips or lose things that were supposed to go home and come back signed.\n\nAnd I remember teachers writing notes or saying things like I talked out of turn, or that I seemed like I couldn’t stay in my seat. At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything, it was just, you know, “she needs to try harder” or “she’s disrupting the class,” but when I think about it now, it feels very connected to what I’m describing. Like the same thread, just in a child version.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you feel this restlessness, is it usually accompanied by a lot of worried or anxious thoughts running through your mind, or is it more just a physical urge to move without much worrying attached to it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more physical, I think. I mean, I can get frustrated about it, because it feels embarrassing that something like sitting down to handle paperwork or talk through household plans can feel so hard, but it doesn’t usually start as a bunch of worried thoughts.

It’s more like my body just won’t settle. Like I’m sitting there and I’m supposed to be calmly going through a form or listening and discussing what needs to happen, and instead I feel this uncomfortable pressure to shift around, get up, move, do something. Then after that I might start thinking, “Why can’t I just do this?” but the first thing is really that restless, crawling-out-of-my-skin feeling.

At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything, it was just, you know, “she needs to try harder” or “she’s disrupting the class,” but when I think about it now, it feels very connected to what I’m describing. Like I’m sitting there and I’m supposed to be calmly going through a form or listening and discussing what needs to happen, and instead I feel this uncomfortable pressure to shift around, get up, move, do something. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely more than one area. I mean, I’m not working right now, so I can’t really answer that in the “at work” sense exactly, but it’s not like it only happens in one specific setting or only with one type of task.

At home it shows up a lot, and the fallout is… honestly kind of painful. People can’t always count on me the way I want them to. I’ll fully intend to follow through on something, and I really do try, but then things slip or I don’t finish them in the way I said I would, and it creates problems.

And it’s also happened in volunteer stuff, too — not just at home. In those circles I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because I kept letting things fall through. Not because I didn’t care, which is the part that bothers me, because I do care. But the end result is still that people can’t reliably depend on me for certain things, and that feels awful and embarrassing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, before 12, definitely. I mean, looking back, it was already showing up in elementary school, not just as a little quirk but in ways that actually caused problems.

I was the kid with the disaster of a desk — papers crumpled and shoved in random places, things I was supposed to bring home just disappearing into the mess. Permission slips were a big one. I’d forget them, or lose them, or remember them at the last second and then feel awful because it became this whole issue at home or with the teacher.

And teachers did notice. I remember getting comments or notes about talking when I wasn’t supposed to, interrupting, or seeming like I couldn’t stay in my seat. At the time it was treated more like I was being careless or not trying hard enough, but yes, it was affecting school and home before I was 12. Looking back now, it feels very obvious that it wasn’t something that suddenly appeared later.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because nothing really stands out to me as “serious trouble” in that way. I mean, I definitely got in trouble as a kid, but when I think of it, it was more the kind of trouble that came from blurting things out, not following along, losing things, being messy, or not doing what I was supposed to do because I’d gotten distracted.

Permission slips were a big one. I’d forget them, or lose them, or remember them at the last second and then feel awful because it became this whole issue at home or with the teacher.\n\nAnd teachers did notice. I remember getting comments or notes about talking when I wasn’t supposed to, interrupting, or seeming like I couldn’t stay in my seat. At the time it was treated more like I was being careless or not trying hard enough, but yes, it was affecting school and home before I was 12. Looking back now, it feels very obvious that it wasn’t something that suddenly appeared later.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did you ever get into serious trouble for things like fighting, being aggressive, breaking rules, or defying authority figures like teachers or parents, beyond just the restlessness and forgetfulness you've described?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because nothing really stands out to me as “serious trouble” in that way. At least that’s not how I remember it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not beyond the kinds of issues I already described. If there were isolated incidents, they’re not something I remember as a pattern or a major part of my childhood.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these periods of restlessness, have you also noticed your mood becoming unusually elevated, euphoric, or irritable, or needing much less sleep than usual while still feeling energetic?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re describing it. I mean, I get restless and wound up, but I wouldn’t say it comes with feeling euphoric or unusually “up” or like I’m suddenly energized in a good way.

The irritability part is harder for me to answer, because I can get irritated when I’m overwhelmed or when I can’t settle myself, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like a distinct mood shift where I’m a different version of myself or anything.

And needing much less sleep but still feeling energetic — no, I haven’t really noticed that. If I sleep less, I usually feel it. I’m not suddenly fine on hardly any sleep. So overall, I don’t think those restless periods are paired with that kind of elevated mood or reduced need for sleep.

{
  "diagnosis": "F90.2",
  "candidates": ["F90.2", "F90.0", "F41.1"],
  "reason": "The patient describes a lifelong pattern of both hyperactive/restless symptoms (inability to sit still, need to move, fidgeting) and inattentive symptoms (losing paperwork, forgetting permission slips, disorganization, failing to follow through on tasks) that began before age 12 and have persisted into adulthood. Symptoms are pervasive across multiple settings (home, volunteer work) and cause functional impairment (unreliable follow-through, embarrassment, strained relationships). Medication-induced akathisia was ruled out (no recent antipsychotic/antiemetic use), anxiety-driven restlessness was ruled out (physical urge to move precedes any worry), and a manic/hypomanic episode was ruled out (no euphoria, no decreased need for sleep, no distinct mood elevation). While the patient denies significant aggressive, defiant, or rule-breaking behavior, the persistent hyperactive-impulsive and inattentive presentation combined with childhood onset and cross-situational impairment supports a hyperkinetic disorder classification (F90.2) as the best fit among the provided diagnostic framework, differentiating from purely inattentive-predominant (F90.0) or anxiety-based (F41.1) presentations.",

(Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI guess what brought me in is… I just feel like I can’t settle in my own body anymore. It’s not even always that the task is hard, exactly — it’s more like my body won’t cooperate with the idea of staying still and calmly handling it.\n\nAnd it’s been bothering me because those are normal adult things, you know? Sitting down, sorting through forms, talking through plans at home, making decisions without feeling like I’m crawling out of my skin. Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The question is asking about a history of serious aggression, rule-breaking, or defiance toward authority figures, which is not covered by the provided ADHD symptom profile items.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
